Table 1.
Actions of centralizers of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triples of nilpotent elements in simple Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}
of classical types on
\mathfrak{g}_{d}

4.2.
Integrable triples in
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
and
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}

4.2.1.
Setup and preliminary results

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
or
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
.
Let
f
be a nilpotent element of
\mathfrak{g}
in Jordan form and let
\boldsymbol{p}
be the associated partition (
4.1
) of
N
,
given by the sizes of the blocks of the Jordan form.
We associate to
\boldsymbol{p}
a symmetric (with respect to the
y
-axis) pyramid,
with boxes of size
2\times 2
indexed by the set
I=\{1,2,\dots,N\}
(say starting from right to left
and bottom to top).
For example, for the partition
(9,7,4^{(2)})
of
24
, we have the pyramid in Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
Symmetric pyramid for the partition
(9,7,4^{(2)})
of
24

Let
V
be the
N
-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}
with basis

\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
.
The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}\cong\mathfrak{gl}(V)
has a basis
consisting of the elementary matrices
E_{\alpha,\beta}
,
\alpha,\beta\in I
.
The elementary matrix
E_{\alpha,\beta}
in
\mathfrak{g}
can be depicted by an arrow
going from the center of the box
\beta
to the center of the box
\alpha
.
In particular,
f
is the “shift to the left” operator. It is depicted as the sum of all the arrows pointing
from each box to the next one on the left

(4.3)

f=\sum_{\alpha\leftarrow\beta}E_{\alpha,\beta}\,,

where the sum is over all adjacent boxes (on the same row)
\alpha,\beta\in I
.
Let us also denote by
f^{\intercal}
the transpose of the matrix
f
defined in (
4.3
). It is the “shift to the right” operator.

Let
h\in\mathfrak{g}
be the diagonal endomorphism of
V

whose eigenvalue on
e_{\alpha}
is the
x
-coordinate of the center of the box labeled by
\alpha
(see Figure
1
)
which we denote by
x_{\alpha}
.
We then have the corresponding
h
-eigenspace decomposition of
V

(4.4)

V=\bigoplus_{k=-D}^{D}V[k]\,,\quad V[k]=\{v\in V\mid h(v)=kv\}\,,

where
D=p_{1}-1
is the maximal eigenvalue of
h
.

We note that the elements
f
and
h
belong to a
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple
\mathfrak{s}=\{e,h,f\}
,
where
e=\sum_{\alpha\leftarrow\beta}c_{\beta}E_{\beta,\alpha}
, with
c_{\beta}=\sum x_{\gamma}
, where the sum is over all boxes
\gamma
at the right and in the same row of the box
\beta
, including it.

The elementary matrices
E_{\alpha,\beta}
are eigenvectors
with respect to the adjoint action of
h
:

(\ad h)E_{\alpha,\beta}=(x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta})E_{\alpha,\beta}\,.

This defines a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of
\mathfrak{g}
,
given by the
\ad h
-eigenspaces as in (
1.1
):

(4.5)

\mathfrak{g}_{k}=\Span_{\mathbb{F}}\{E_{\alpha,\beta}\mid x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}=k\}\,,\,k\in\mathbb{Z}.

The depth of this grading is
d=2D=2p_{1}-2
.

Next, consider the subspaces
V_{-}=\Ker f
and
V_{+}=\Ker f^{\intercal}
of
V
.
We thus have the direct sums decompositions

(4.6)

V=V_{-}\oplus f^{\intercal}V=V_{+}\oplus fV\,.

Let
D_{i}=p_{i}-1
, for
i=1,\dots,s
(in particular
D_{1}=D
). Throughout the paper we will use the decompositions

V_{\pm}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}V_{\pm,i}
,
where

(4.7)

V_{-,i}=V_{-}\cap f^{D_{i}}V_{+}\,\,,\,\,\,\,V_{+,i}=V_{+}\cap(f^{\intercal})^{D_{i}}V_{-}\,\,,\,\,\,\,i=1,\dots,s\,,

and

V=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{s}V_{i}
,
where

(4.8)

V_{i}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{D_{i}}f^{k}V_{+,i}\,\,,\,\,\,\,i=1,\dots,s\,.

Representing the basis elements of
V
as boxes of the pyramid as in Figure
1
,
V_{i}
corresponds to the
i
-th rectangle counting from the bottom, and
V_{\pm,i}
correspond to the
right/left most boxes of the
i
-th rectangle.
With a picture:

V_{-,1}
V_{1}
V_{+,1}
V_{-,2}
V_{2}
V_{+,2}
V_{-,3}
V_{3}
V_{+,3}

Figure 2.
The spaces
V_{i}
and
V_{\pm,i}

For the pyramid in Figure
2
the subspaces
V_{-}
and
V_{+}
correspond, respectively, to the boxes colored in orange and blue (note that they may have nontrivial intersection); the subspaces
V_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, correspond to the rectangles of the pyramid, and
V_{\pm,i}
is the intersection of the rectangle
V_{i}
with
V_{\pm}
.

Throughout the paper, given a subspace
U\subset V
,
together with a “natural” splitting
V=U\oplus W

(usually associated with the grading of
V
),
we shall denote, with a slight abuse of notation,
by
\mathbbm{1}_{U}

both the identity map
U\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}U
,
the inclusion map
U\hookrightarrow V
,
and the projection map (with kernel
W
)
V\twoheadrightarrow U
;
the correct meaning of
\mathbbm{1}_{U}
should then be clear from the context.

Using the above notation, we clearly have (recall the splitting (
4.6
))

(4.9)

ff^{\intercal}=\mathbbm{1}_{V}-\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}=\mathbbm{1}_{fV}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,f^{\intercal}f=\mathbbm{1}_{V}-\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}=\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}\,.

The following result will be used in the sequel.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
x\in\mathfrak{g}_{-2}^{f}
, the centralizer of
f
in
\mathfrak{g}_{-2}
. For every
i=1,\dots,s
there exists
t_{i}\in\End(V_{+,1})
such that

\mathbbm{1}_{V_{i}}x\mathbbm{1}_{V_{i}}=\sum_{k=0}^{D_{i}-1}f^{k+1}t_{i}(f^{\intercal})^{k}\,.

Proof.

By the direct sum (
4.8
) we can write
\mathbbm{1}_{V_{i}}x\mathbbm{1}_{V_{i}}=\sum_{k=0}^{D_{i}-1}x_{k}
, where
x_{k}\in\Hom(f^{k}V_{+,i},f^{k+1}V_{+,i})
.
The condition
[x,f]=0
gives

(4.10)

fx_{k}=x_{k+1}f\in\Hom(f^{k}V_{+,i},f^{k+2}V_{+,i})\,,

for every
k=0,\dots,D_{i}-2
.
Multiplying both sides of (
4.10
) on the right by
f^{\intercal}
and using (
4.9
)
we get

fx_{k}f^{\intercal}=x_{k+1}\mathbbm{1}_{fV}=x_{k+1}\,,

for
k=0,\dots,D_{i}-2
. A recursive solution to these equations is

x_{k}=f^{k}x_{0}(f^{\intercal})^{k}\,,\qquad k=1,\dots,D_{i}-1\,.

Letting
t_{i}=f^{\intercal}x_{0}\in\End(V_{+,i})
we get the claim.
∎

In order to apply, in the following sections, Proposition
3.13
we need the following result.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
x\in\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
and assume that
0\neq x\in\Hom(V_{j},V_{i})
. Then

(4.11)

(\ad f)^{-1}x=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}xf^{k}\,,}&\displaystyle{i<j\,,}\\
\displaystyle{-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}f^{k}x(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}\,,}&\displaystyle{i>j\,.}\end{array}\right.

(Note that, since
x\in\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
, it must be
i\neq j
.)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let us assume that

x\in\Hom(f^{k_{j}}V_{+,j},f^{k_{i}}V_{+,i})
.
Note that, since
x\in\mathfrak{g}_{-1}
, we have

(4.12)

D_{i}-2k_{i}=D_{j}-2k_{j}-1\,.

In particular, if
i<j
, then
D_{i}>D_{j}
and therefore
k_{i}>k_{j}
. Conversely, if
i>j
, then

D_{i}<D_{j}
and
k_{i}<k_{j}
. Moreover,
D_{j}-k_{j}>D_{i}-k_{i}
.

First, let us assume that
i<j
. Applying
\ad f
to the RHS of equation (
4.11
)
we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}f(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}xf^{k}-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}xf^{k}f

\displaystyle=x-\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}(f^{\intercal})^{k}xf^{k}=x-(f^{\intercal})^{k_{i}}xf^{k_{i}}\,.

In the first equality we used the first equation in (
4.9
),
while, for the second equality, the operator
\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}
on the left forces
k=k_{i}
.
Since
i<j
, by the observation after equation (
4.12
) we have
k_{i}>k_{j}
. Hence,
xf^{k_{i}}=0
thus proving (
4.11
) in this case.

Next, let us assume that
i>j
and let us apply
\ad f
to the RHS of equation (
4.11
):

\displaystyle-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}ff^{k}x(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}f^{k}x(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}f

\displaystyle=x-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}f^{k}x(f^{\intercal})^{k}\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}=x-f^{D_{j}-k_{j}}x(f^{\intercal})^{D_{j}-k_{j}}\,.

In the first equality we used the second equation in (
4.9
),
while, for the second equality, the operator
\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}
on the right forces
k=D_{j}-k_{j}
.
Since
i>j
, by the observation after equation (
4.12
) we have
D_{j}-k_{j}>D_{i}-k_{i}
. Hence,
f^{D_{j}-k_{j}}x=0
. This completes the proof.
∎

The next result will be used in Sections
4.2.3
and
4.4.2
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
U\in\Hom(V[D],V[-D])
and
E=A+B
, where
A\in\Hom(V[-D+1],V[D])
and

B\in\Hom(V[-D],V[D-1])
. Then,
\ad E\circ(\ad f)^{-1}\circ\ad E(U)=0
if and only if

[Af^{D-1}Bf^{D},(f^{\intercal})^{D}U]=0.

Proof.

We have that
[E,U]=UA-BU
. Since
UA\in\Hom(V_{2},V_{1})
and
BU\in\Hom(V_{1},V_{2})
,
using Lemma
4.2
we have that

\displaystyle\left(\ad E\circ(\ad f)^{-1}\circ\ad E\right)(U)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}[E,(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}UAf^{k}+f^{k}BU(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}]

\displaystyle=Af^{D-1}BU(f^{\intercal})^{D}-(f^{\intercal})^{D}UAf^{D-1}B\,.

The claim follows from the fact that
(f^{\intercal})^{D}:V[-D]\to V[D]
is an isomorphism with inverse
f^{D}
.
∎

The next result will be used in Sections
4.2.3
and
5.1
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
U=X+Y
, where
X\in\Hom(V[D],V[-D+1])
,
Y\in\Hom(V[D-1],V[-D])
, and let
E=A+B+C
,
where
A\in\Hom(V[-D],V[D-2])
,
B\in\Hom(V[-D+2],V[D])
and
C\in\Hom(V[-D+1],V[D-1])
. Then,
\ad E\circ(\ad f)^{-1}\circ\ad E(U)=0
if and only if

(4.13)

\begin{split}&(f^{\intercal})^{D}YCf^{D-1}C=(f^{\intercal}\mathbbm{1}_{fV}A+B\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}f^{\intercal})YC+B\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}f^{D-2}\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}AY\,,\\
&Cf^{D-1}CX(f^{\intercal})^{D}=CX(f^{\intercal}\mathbbm{1}_{fV}A+B\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}f^{\intercal})+(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}XB\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}f^{D-2}\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}A\,.\end{split}

Proof.

Writing
A=\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}A+\mathbbm{1}_{fV}A
and

B=B_{\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}}+B\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}
,
we have that
[E,U]=\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}AY+BX-XB\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}+\mathbbm{1}_{fV}AY-YB-XB\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}
.
Since
\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}AY+BX-XB\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}\in\oplus_{i=1,2}\Hom(V_{i},V_{i+1})
and
\mathbbm{1}_{fV}AY-YB-XB\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}\in\oplus_{i=1,2}\Hom(V_{i+1},V_{i})
,
using Lemma
4.2
we have that

(4.14)

\begin{split}&\left((\ad f)^{-1}\circ\ad E\right)(U)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}(\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}AY+CX-XB\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V})f^{k}\\
&-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}f^{k}(\mathbbm{1}_{fV}AY-YC-XB\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}})(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}\,.\end{split}

Finally, applying
\ad E
to both side of equation (
4.14
) we get

(4.15)

\begin{split}&\left(\ad E\circ(\ad f)^{-1}\circ\ad E\right)(U)=-B\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}f^{\intercal}YC-B\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}f^{D-1}\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}AY(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}\\
&-Cf^{D-1}CX(f^{\intercal})^{D}+CXB\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}f^{\intercal}+(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}XB\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}f^{D-2}\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}A\\
&-f^{\intercal}\mathbbm{1}_{fV}AYC+(f^{\intercal})^{D}YCf^{D-1}C+CXf^{\intercal}\mathbbm{1}_{fV}A\,.\end{split}

Note that

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle{B\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}f^{\intercal}YB+B\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}f^{D-1}\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}AY(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}}\\
\displaystyle{+f^{\intercal}\mathbbm{1}_{fV}AYC-(f^{\intercal})^{D}YCf^{D-1}C\in\Hom(V[-D+1],V[D])}\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle{Cf^{D-1}CX(f^{\intercal})^{D}-CXB\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}f^{\intercal}-(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}XB\mathbbm{1}_{V_{-}}f^{D-2}\mathbbm{1}_{V_{+}}A}\\
\displaystyle{-CXf^{\intercal}\in\Hom(V[-D],V[D-1])\,.}\end{array}

Hence, the RHS of (
4.15
) vanishes if and only if equation (
4.13
) holds.
∎

Finally, the last result of this section will be used in Section
4.5.2
..

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
U=X+Y
, where
X\in\Hom(V[D],V[-D+2])
,
Y\in\Hom(V[D-2],V[-D])
, and let
E=A+B
,
where
A\in\Hom(V[-D],V[D-3])
,
B\in\Hom(V[-D+3],V[D])
.
Let us assume that

(4.16)

\mathbbm{1}_{fV}A=0\,,\qquad B\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}=0\,.

Then,
\ad E\circ(\ad f)^{-1}\circ\ad E(U)=0
if and only if

(f^{\intercal})^{D-2}XBf^{D-3}A=Bf^{D-3}AY(f^{\intercal})^{D-2}=0\,.

Proof.

Note that
[E,U]=AY-XB
. Using the first equation in (
4.16
) and (
4.11
)
we have that

(\ad f)^{-1}(AY)=-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}f^{k}AY(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}\,,

while using the second equation in (
4.16
) and (
4.11
) we have that

(\ad f)^{-1}(XB)=-\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}(f^{\intercal})^{k+1}XBf^{k}\,.

Hence, by a straightforward computation we get

\ad E\circ(\ad f)^{-1}\ad E(U)=-Bf^{D-3}AY(f^{\intercal})^{D-2}+(f^{\intercal})^{D-2}XBf^{D-3}A\,.

Equation (
4.16
) follows since

Bf^{D-3}AY(f^{\intercal})^{D-2}\in\Hom(V[-D+2],V[D])
and

(f^{\intercal})^{D-2}XBf^{D-3}A\in\Hom(V[-D],V[D-2])
.
∎

4.2.2.
The centralizer
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})

From (
4.5
) we have that a homogeneous element
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, has the form

(4.17)

E=\sum_{x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}=k}c_{\alpha\beta}E_{\alpha\beta}\,,\quad c_{\alpha\beta}\in\mathbb{F}\,.

The goal of this section is to describe the centralizer
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})
of

\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2}
in
\mathfrak{g}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

If
[E,\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2}]=0
, then
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus W\oplus\mathbb{F}\mathbbm{1}_{V}
, where

W=\Hom(V[-D+1],V[D-1])\subset\mathfrak{g}_{d-2}\,.

Proof.

Since the adjoint action of
e\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}
is injective on
\mathfrak{g}_{<0}
, we obviously have

\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 0}
. Let
\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}\in I
be such that
x_{\tilde{\alpha}}=D
and

x_{\tilde{\beta}}\leqslant D-2
(i. e. the box
\tilde{\beta}
is completely at the left of the box
\tilde{\alpha}
).
Then
E_{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2}
. Hence, letting
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{k}
be as in equation (
4.17
), we have

(4.18)

0=[E_{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}},E]=\sum_{x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}=k}c_{\alpha\beta}[E_{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}},E_{\alpha\beta}]=\sum_{x_{\tilde{\beta}}-x_{\beta}=k}c_{\tilde{\beta}\beta}E_{\tilde{\alpha}\beta}-\sum_{x_{\alpha}-D=k}c_{\alpha\tilde{\alpha}}E_{\alpha\tilde{\beta}}\,.

If
k\geqslant 1
, then the condition
x_{\alpha}-D=k\geqslant 1
implies that
x_{\alpha}\geqslant 1+D
thus the second sum
in (
4.18
) is empty. Hence, from equation (
4.18
) we have that
c_{\alpha\beta}=0
if
x_{\alpha}\leqslant D-2
.
If
k=0
, then equation (
4.18
) becomes

0=\sum_{x_{\tilde{\beta}}=x_{\beta}}c_{\tilde{\beta}\beta}E_{\tilde{\alpha}\beta}-\sum_{x_{\alpha}=D}c_{\alpha\tilde{\alpha}}E_{\alpha\tilde{\beta}}\,,

from which follows that, for
x_{\alpha},x_{\beta}\neq D-1
, we have
c_{\alpha,\beta}=\delta_{\alpha\beta}\lambda
, for some

\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
.
Similarly, letting
\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}\in I
be such that
x_{\tilde{\alpha}}=-D
and
x_{\tilde{\beta}}\geqslant-D+2

(i. e. the box
\tilde{\beta}
is completely at the right of the box
\tilde{\alpha}
), the
condition
[E,E_{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}}]=0
implies, for
k\geqslant 1
, that
c_{\alpha\beta}=0
if
x_{\beta}\geqslant-D+2

and, for
k=0
, that
c_{\alpha,\beta}=\delta_{\alpha\beta}\lambda
for
x_{\alpha},x_{\beta}\neq-D+1
.
This proves that

E\in\Span_{\mathbb{F}}\{\mathbbm{1}_{V},E_{\alpha\beta}\mid x_{\alpha}\geqslant D-1,x_{\beta}\leqslant-D+1\}=\mathfrak{g}_{d}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus W\oplus\mathbb{F}\mathbbm{1}_{V}\,.

∎

Proposition 4.7
.

The centralizer of
\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2}
in
\mathfrak{g}
is
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})=\mathfrak{g}_{d}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus W\oplus\mathbb{F}\mathbbm{1}_{V}
.

Proof.

By degree consideration and the fact that the identity is a central element we clearly have

\mathfrak{g}_{d}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathbb{F}\mathbbm{1}_{V}\subset\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})
.
Let
T\in\Hom(V[-D+1],V[D-1])
, and let
E_{\alpha\beta}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2}
, with
x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}\geqslant 2
.
In particular,
x_{\alpha}\geqslant x_{\beta}+2>-D+1
, and
x_{\beta}\leqslant x_{\alpha}-2<D-1
.
Hence,
\im(E_{\alpha\beta})\cap V[-D+1]=0
, so that
TE_{\alpha\beta}=0
. Similarly,

\im(T)\subset\Ker(E_{\alpha\beta})
, so that
E_{\alpha\beta}T=0
. Hence,
T\in\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})
.
This, combined to Lemma
4.6
, completes the proof.
∎

Recalling the definition of integrable triples given in the Introduction, if

E
is an integrable element for
f
, then

E\in\left(W\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d}\right)\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 1}
(note that
\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 1}
for
p_{1}\geqslant 2
, and
W\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 1}
for
p_{1}\geqslant 3
).

4.2.3.
The coisotropy condition

An element
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
can be uniquely decomposed as
E=a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}+b(f^{\intercal})^{D}
, where

(4.19)

a\in\Hom(V[D-1],V[D])\quad\text{ and }\quad b\in\Hom(V[D],V[D-1]).

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
E=a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}+b(f^{\intercal})^{D}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
. The subspace
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is
coisotropic with respect to the bilinear form (
1.2
) if and only if

(4.20)

ab=\lambda\mathbbm{1}_{V[D]}\,,\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{F}\,.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.3
with
A=a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}
and
B=b(f^{\intercal})^{D}
we have that

\ad E\circ(\ad f)^{-1}\circ\ad E|_{\mathfrak{g}_{-d}}=0
if and only if

(4.21)

[ab,(f^{\intercal})^{D}U]=0\,,

for every
U\in\mathfrak{g}_{-d}
. Note that
(f^{\intercal})^{D}\mathfrak{g}_{-d}\cong\End(V[D])
. Hence, equation (
4.21
)
implies that
ab
is a scalar. The claim follows from Proposition
3.13
.
∎

An element
E\in W\subset\mathfrak{g}_{d-2}
can be uniquely written as
E=c(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}
, where

(4.22)

c\in\End(V[D-1]).

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
E=c(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}\in W
. The subspace
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is
coisotropic with respect to the bilinear form (
1.2
) if and only if

c^{2}=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.4
with
A=B=0
and
C=c(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}
we have that

\ad E\circ(\ad f)^{-1}\circ\ad E|_{\mathfrak{g}_{-d+1}}=0
if and only if
c^{2}(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}X(f^{\intercal})^{D}=0
, for every
X\in\Hom(V[D],V[-D+1])
. Since
(f^{\intercal})^{D}:V[-D]\to V[D]

and

(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}:V[-D+1]\to V[D-1]
are isomorphisms, this condition is the same as
c^{2}=0
. The claim follows from Proposition
3.13
.
∎

4.2.4.
Integrable
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}

Let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}
.
In this section we will use the decomposition (see (
4.8
))

V=V_{1}\oplus V_{\geqslant 2}\,,\quad\text{where }V_{\geqslant 2}=\bigoplus_{i=2}^{s}V_{i}\,.

Note that
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 2}}=f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 2}}
. Hence,
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 2}}
is nilpotent.
Furthermore, note that
E
can be uniquely written as
E=u(f^{\intercal})^{D}
, where
u\in\End(V[D])
.
Let

U=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}f^{k}\im u\subset V_{1}\,,\quad\tilde{U}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}f^{k}\Ker u\subset V_{1}\,.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
E=u(f^{\intercal})^{D}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}
. If
u
is semisimple, then the nilpotent part of
f+E
is

(f+E)_{n}=f\mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{U}\oplus V_{\geqslant 2}}
.

Proof.

Recall that
V_{+,1}=V[D]
(see (
4.7
) and Figure
2
).
Since
u
is semisimple, we have that
V[D]=\Ker u\oplus\im u
. This implies, by (
4.8
),

V_{1}=U\oplus\tilde{U}
.

Clearly,
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{U}\oplus V_{\geqslant 2}}=f\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{U}\oplus V_{\geqslant 2}}

and
(f+E)U\subset U
.
Denote
A=(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{U}
. Clearly, Since
A
commutes with
f\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{U}\oplus V_{\geqslant 2}}

and
f+E=A+f\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{U}\oplus V_{\geqslant 2}}
.
We claim that
A
is semisimple so that the semisimple part of
f+E
is
(f+E)_{s}=A
and

(f+E)_{n}=f\mathbbm{1}_{\widetilde{U}\oplus V_{\geqslant 2}}
.
Let
q(x)
be the minimal polynomial of
u\mathbbm{1}_{\im u}
which has
distinct non-zero roots in
\mathbb{F}
, since
u
is semisimple.
Let
\tilde{q}(x)=q(x^{p_{1}})
which has also distinct roots.
Note that, if
v\in\im u
, then
A^{kp_{1}}f^{h}v=f^{h}u^{k}v
, for every
h,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
Hence,
\tilde{q}(A)f^{h}v=q(A^{p_{1}})f^{h}v=f^{h}q(u)v=0
. This implies that the minimal polynomial of
A
divides

\tilde{q}(x)
. Since
\tilde{q}(x)
has distinct roots,
A
is semisimple, as claimed.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

If
E=u(f^{\intercal})^{D}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}
is integrable for
f
, then
u
is semisimple.

Proof.

Note that
f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 2}}
is nilpotent and commutes with
f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}}+E=(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}}
. Hence,
for the Jordan decomposition of
f+E
we have

(f+E)_{s}=(f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}}+E)_{s}\quad\text{and}\quad(f+E)_{n}=(f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}}+E)_{n}+f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 2}}\,.

Since, by assumptions,
f+E
is integrable, we then have

(f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}}+E)_{s}=f_{1}+E\quad\text{and}\quad(f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}}+E)_{n}=\tilde{f}_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-2}^{f}\cap\End(V_{1})\,,

with
f_{1}+\tilde{f}_{2}=f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}}
.
By Lemma
4.1
, we have that

(4.23)

\tilde{f}_{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{D-1}f^{k+1}t(f^{\intercal})^{k}\,,

for some
t\in\End(V[D])
.
Moreover, by Definition (I
3
) we have that
[E,\tilde{f}_{2}]=E\tilde{f}_{2}-\tilde{f}_{2}E=0
. Since
E\tilde{f}_{2}\in\Hom(V[-D+2],V[D])

and
\tilde{f}_{2}E\in\Hom(V[-D],V[D-2])
, we have that
E\tilde{f}_{2}=\tilde{f}_{2}E=0
. Explicitly, using equation (
4.23
) we get

E\tilde{f}_{2}=Ef^{D}t(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}=ut(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}=0
,
which implies

(4.24)

ut=0\,.

Let
A=f_{1}+E
and let
v\in V[D]
.
We have, by equation (
4.23
),
Av=(f+E-\tilde{f}_{2})v=fv-\tilde{f}_{2}v=f(1-t)v
,
and repeating the same computation
k
times,

(4.25)

A^{k}v=f^{k}(1-t)^{k}v\,,\quad 0\leqslant k\leqslant D\,.

Letting
k=D=p_{1}-1
in (
4.25
), and applying
A
one more time, we get

(4.26)

A^{p_{1}}v=(f_{1}+E)f^{D}(1-t)^{D}v=u(1-t)^{D}v=uv\,.

For the last equality we used (
4.24
).
Since
A
is semisimple,
A^{p_{1}}
is semisimple as well. Moreover, from equation
(
4.26
) we have that
A^{p_{1}}V[D]\subset V[D]
and
A^{p_{1}}\mathbbm{1}_{V[D]}=u
. As a consequence,

u
is semisimple, proving the claim.
∎

Combining Lemmas
4.10
and
4.11
we get the following result.

Proposition 4.12
.

E=u(f^{\intercal})^{D}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}
is integrable for
f
if and only if
u
is semisimple.

Remark 4.13
.

Proposition
4.12
is in accordance with Theorem
3.15
and the results of Section

4.1.1
: it is well-known that the closed orbits for the action of
SL_{r_{1}}
on
\Mat_{r_{1}\times r_{1}}

by conjugation are indeed the semisimple elements of
\Mat_{r_{1}\times r_{1}}
.

4.2.5.
Integrable
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}

In this subsection we will use the decomposition (see (
4.8
))
V=V_{\leqslant 2}\oplus V_{\geqslant 3}
,
where
V_{\leqslant 2}=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
and
V_{\geqslant 3}=\bigoplus_{i=3}^{s}V_{i}
.

Let, as in (
4.19
),
E=a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}+b(f^{\intercal})^{D}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
, where

a\in\Hom(V[D-1],V[D])
and
b\in\Hom(V[D],V[D-1])
.
Since
V[D]\subset V_{1}
and
V[D-1]\subset V_{2}
, we have
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 3}}=f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 3}}
, which is
nilpotent.
Let us also denote by

U=V_{1}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}f^{k}\im b\right)\subset V_{\leqslant 2}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{U}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}f^{k}\Ker a\subset V_{2}\,.

Proposition 4.14
.

An element
E=a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}+b(f^{\intercal})^{D}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
is
integrable for
f
if and only if
ab=\lambda\mathbbm{1}_{V[D]}
,
\lambda\neq 0
.

Proof.

First, assume that
ab=\lambda\mathbbm{1}_{V[D]}
, with
\lambda\neq 0
.
Let
u\in V[D-1]=V_{+,2}
(see (
4.7
) and Figure
2
). Clearly,
u=\frac{1}{\lambda}bau+(u-\frac{1}{\lambda}bau)\in\im b+\Ker a
.
Moreover, if
u\in\im b\cap\Ker a
, then
au
=0 and
u=bw
, for some
w\in V[D]
, so that
0=au=abw=\lambda w

which implies
w=0
, and hence
u=0
. This shows that
V[D-1]=\im b\oplus\Ker a
and, by (
4.8
), that

V_{\leqslant 2}=U\oplus\tilde{U}
.
Clearly,
f+E=(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{U}+(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{U}\oplus V_{\geqslant 3}}
.
If
v\in V[D]\subset U
, then

\displaystyle(f+E)^{D}v=f^{D}v\,,\quad(f+E)^{D+1}v=Ef^{D}v=b(f^{\intercal})^{D}f^{D}v=bv\,,

\displaystyle(f+E)^{2D}v=f^{D-1}bv\,,\quad(f+E)^{2D+1}v=Ef^{D-1}bv=a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}f^{D-1}v=abv=\lambda v\,.

Similarly, one can check that for every
v\in U
we have
(f+E)^{2D+1}v=\lambda v
.
Since
\lambda\neq 0
,
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{U}=f\mathbbm{1}_{U}+E
is semisimple. Furthermore, we clearly have

(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{U}\oplus V_{\geqslant 3}}=f\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{U}\oplus V_{\geqslant 3}}\,,

which is nilpotent and commutes with
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{U}
.
Hence, the Jordan decomposition of
f+E
is

(f+E)_{s}=f\mathbbm{1}_{U}+E\,,\quad(f+E)_{n}=f\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{U}\oplus V_{\geqslant 3}}\,,

which omplies that
f+E
is integrable.

Conversely, if
f+E
is integrable, in particular
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
must be coisotropic. Hence, by Proposition

4.8
,
ab=\lambda\mathbbm{1}_{V[D]}
, for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
. On the other hand, it must be
\lambda\neq 0

otherwise, as one can easily check,
f+E
is nilpotent.
∎

The next result will be used in Section
4.5.1
. We state and prove it here since we need the notation introduced here.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
E=a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}+b(f^{\intercal})^{D}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
. Assume that
f+E
is not nilpotent and that its nilpotent part lies in
\mathfrak{g}_{-2}
.
Then
ab\in\End(V[D])
is a non-zero semisimple element.

Proof.

Note that
f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 3}}
is nilpotent and commutes with
f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\leqslant 2}}+E=(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\leqslant 2}}
. Hence,
for the Jordan decomposition of
f+E
we have

(f+E)_{s}=(f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\leqslant 2}}+E)_{s}\quad\text{and}\quad(f+E)_{n}=(f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\leqslant 2}}+E)_{n}+f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 3}}\,.

By assumption,
\tilde{f}_{2}=(f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\leqslant 2}}+E)_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-2}
. In particular,

\tilde{f}_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-2}^{f}\cap(\End(V_{1})\oplus\End(V_{2}))
.
Hence, by Lemma
4.1
, we have that

(4.27)

\tilde{f}_{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{D-1}f^{k+1}(t+s)(f^{\intercal})^{k}\,,

for some
t\in\End(V[D])
and
s\in\End(V[D-1])
.
Moreover, by Definition (I
3
) we have that
[E,\tilde{f}_{2}]=\left(a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}+b(f^{\intercal})^{D}\right)\tilde{f}_{2}-\tilde{f}_{2}\left(a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}+b(f^{\intercal})^{D}\right))=0
. Since

a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}\tilde{f}_{2}\in\Hom(V[-D+3],V[D])
,
b(f^{\intercal})^{D}\tilde{f}_{2}\in\Hom(V[-D+2],V[D-1])
,
\tilde{f}_{2}a\in\Hom(V[D-1],V[D-2])

and
\tilde{f}_{2}b\in\Hom(V[D],V[D-3])
, we have
a(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}\tilde{f}_{2}=b(f^{\intercal})^{D}\tilde{f}_{2}=\tilde{f}_{2}a=\tilde{f}_{2}b=0
. Explicitly, using equation (
4.27
) we get

b(f^{\intercal})^{D}\tilde{f}_{2}=bt(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}=0
,
which implies

(4.28)

bt=0\,.

Moreover, using again equation (
4.27
) we get

\tilde{f}_{2}b=fsb=0
,
which implies

(4.29)

sb=0\,.

Next, let
A=(f+E)_{s}
and let
v\in V[D]
. We have,
by equation (
4.27
),

Av=(f+E-\tilde{f}_{2})v=fv-\tilde{f}_{2}v=f(1-t)v\,.

Repeating the same computation
D
times, we get (cf. equation (
4.25
))
A^{D}v=f^{D}v
,
and applying
A
one more time we get

(4.30)

A^{D+1}v=Ef^{D}(1-t)^{D}v=b(1-t)^{D}v=bv\,.

For the last equality we used (
4.28
).
By equations (
4.27
) and (
4.30
) we have

A^{D+2}v=(f+E-\tilde{f}_{2})bv=fbv-fsbv=f(1-s)bv=fbv\,,

where in the last equality we used (
4.29
).
Repeating the same computation
D-1
times we get

A^{2D}v=f^{D-1}bv\,.

Applying
A
again, we finally get

(4.31)

A^{2D+1}v=Ef^{D-1}bv=abv\,.

Since
A
is non-zero semisimple,
A^{2D+1}
is non-zero semisimple as well. Moreover, from equation
(
4.31
) we have that
A^{2D+1}V[D]\subset V[D]
and
A^{2D+1}\mathbbm{1}_{V[D]}=ab
thus showing that

ab
is non-zero semisimple and concluding the proof.
∎

4.2.6.
No integrable elements for
f
in
W

In this subsection we will use the decomposition

(4.32)

V=V_{2}\oplus V_{\neq 2}\,,\quad\text{where }V_{\neq 2}=\bigoplus_{i\neq 2}V_{i}\,.

Proposition 4.16
.

There are no integrable elements for
f
in
W
.

Proof.

By contradiction, let
E=c(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}\in W
, where
c
is as in (
4.22
),
be integrable for
f
. Since
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is coisotropic, by Proposition
4.9
we have
c^{2}=0
.
Clearly,
f+E=(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{2}}+(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\neq 2}}
, and
f+E
preserves the direct sum decomposition (
4.32
).
Note that
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\neq 2}}=f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\neq 2}}
which is nilpotent.
On the other hand, it is not difficult to check that if
v\in V_{2}
, then we have
(f+E)^{2D}v=c^{2}v=0
. Hence,
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{2}}
is nilpotent as well.
This proves that
f+E
is nilpotent, contradicting the fact that it is integrable.
∎

As a consequence of Propositions
4.12
,
4.14
and
4.16
we get the following.

Corollary 4.17
.

If
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{k}
,
k\geqslant 1
, is an integrable element for
f\in\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
or
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
, then
k=d
or
k=d-1
.
In other words,
f+E
is integrable if and only if it is an integrable cyclic or quasicyclic element.

Remark 4.18
.

For
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
, the triple
(0,f,\mathbbm{1}_{V})
satisfies Definition (I
1
),(I
2
) and (I
3
). However,

\mathbbm{1}_{V}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
.

4.2.7.

Here we reformulate the results on quasi-cyclic elements in
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
or
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
in terms of polar linear groups.
First, note that
\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
is naturally identified with the space

(4.33)

\Hom(V[D-1],V[D])\oplus\Hom(V[D],V[D-1])

(recall equation (
4.19
)) with the action of the group
Z(\mathfrak{s})
defined by the natural action of
GL_{r_{2}}\times GL_{r_{1}}

(recall that
\dim V[D]=r_{1}
and
\dim V[D-1]=r_{2}
). This linear group is polar since it is a theta group.

Proposition
4.14
says that an element
E=\varphi\oplus\psi
from (
4.33
) is integrable if and only if

(4.34)

\varphi\circ\psi=\lambda\mathbbm{1}_{r_{1}},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{F},\quad\lambda\neq 0.

It follows that for existence of an integrable
E
in
\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
it is necessary and sufficient that

(4.35)

r_{1}\leqslant r_{2}.

Suppose that condition (
4.35
) holds.
Then for the polar linear group
GL_{r_{2}}\times GL_{r_{1}}
acting on (
4.33
) one can choose a Cartan subspace
C
,
consisting of the following matrices (in some bases of
V[D-1]
and
V[D]
);

C=\left\{(A\ 0)\oplus(A\ 0)^{\intercal}\mid A=\diag(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{r_{1}})\in\Mat_{r_{1}\times r_{1}}\right\}.

Then condition (
4.34
) on an element from
C
means that
\lambda_{1}^{2}=...=\lambda_{r_{1}}^{2}=\lambda^{2}
,
hence
\lambda_{j}=\pm\lambda
for all
j
. Since the Weyl group of
C
contains all sign changes of diagonal elements,
up to the action of the Weyl group and rescaling,
C
contains a unique integrable element
(\mathbbm{1}_{r_{1}}\ 0)\oplus(\mathbbm{1}_{r_{1}}\ 0)^{\intercal}
.
Due to Theorem
3.16
(
b
), any integrable
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
is
Z(\mathfrak{s})
-conjugate to
C
.
Thus we obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 4.19
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{gl}_{N}
or
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
and let
f\in\mathfrak{g}
be a non-zero nilpotent element of depth
d
, corresponding to the partition (
4.1
).

(a)

If there exists an integrable
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{j}
,
j\geqslant 1
, for the nilpotent element
f
, then
j=d
or
d-1
.

(b)

The linear group
Z(\mathfrak{s})|\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
is polar.

(c)

The element
f
admits an integrable element
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
if and only if
r_{1}\leqslant r_{2}
.
Provided that this condition holds, there exists a unique, up to equivalence, integrable element
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
for
f
.

Remark 4.20
.

It follows from
[
EKV13
]
and the above discussion that for a nilpotent element
f\in\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
there exists
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{j}
,
such that the element
f+E
is semisimple if and only if
j=d
and
p_{2}=1
, or
j=d-1
and
r_{1}=r_{2}
and
p_{3}=1
.
This claim was stated in
[
DSKV13
]
, where the associated integrable Hamiltonian systems were also discussed.

4.3.
General setup for symplectic and orthogonal Lie algebras

Recall from
[
CMG93
]
that nilpotent orbits of
\mathfrak{sp}_{N}
(respectively
\mathfrak{so}_{N}
)
are in one-to-one correspondence with partitions
\boldsymbol{p}
of
N

as in (
4.1
) with the property that if
p_{a}
is odd (respectively even), then
r_{a}
is even,
1\leqslant a\leqslant s
.

Let
V
be the
N
-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}
with basis

\{e_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
, where

I
is an index set for the basis, which can be identified with the set of boxes in the pyramid associated to

\boldsymbol{p}
(cf. Fig.
1
).
Given
\alpha\in I
we let
\alpha^{\prime}\in I
correspond to the box in the same rectangle as
\alpha
reflected
with respect to the center of the rectangle. For example, in Fig.
1
, if
\alpha
is the box
labelled by
17
, then
\alpha^{\prime}
is the box labelled by
24
, while if
\alpha
is the box labelled by
23
,
then
\alpha^{\prime}
is the box labelled by
18
.

Clearly,
\alpha^{\prime\prime}=\alpha
. Let
\eta=\pm 1
, and choose a map
\epsilon:I\to\{\pm 1\}

with the properties that

(4.36)

\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\alpha^{\prime}}=\eta\,,\qquad\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}=-1\,,\qquad\text{for }\alpha,\beta\text{ adjacent boxes in the same row}\,.

For example, let
\nu:I\to\{1,2,\dots,N\}
be the ordering of the boxes of the pyramid going from right
to left and then from bottom to top. Then
\epsilon_{\alpha}=(-1)^{\nu(\alpha)}
satisfies
the properties in (
4.36
) with
\eta=(-1)^{N+1}
. It is easy to see that for every choice of

\eta
such a map
\epsilon:I\to\{\pm 1\}
exists.

Let us define on
V
a bilinear form
\langle\cdot\mid\cdot\rangle:V\otimes V\to\mathbb{F}
letting

(4.37)

\langle e_{\alpha}|e_{\beta}\rangle=\epsilon_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha,\beta^{\prime}}\,,\qquad\alpha,\beta\in I\,.

Lemma 4.21
.

The bilinear form (
4.37
) satisfies (
v,w\in V
)

\langle v|w\rangle=\eta\langle w|v\rangle\,.

Proof.

From equations (
4.36
) and (
4.37
) we have

\langle e_{\beta}|e_{\alpha}\rangle=\epsilon_{\beta}\delta_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta}=\epsilon_{\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta}=\eta\epsilon_{\alpha}\delta_{\alpha,\beta^{\prime}}=\eta\langle e_{\alpha}|e_{\beta}\rangle\,,\qquad\alpha,\beta\in I\,.

∎

Given
A\in\End V
, let us denote by
A^{\dagger}
its adjoint with respect to the bilinear form (
4.37
). By Lemma

4.21
we have that

(4.38)

\mathfrak{g}=\{A\in\End V\mid A=-A^{\dagger}\}\simeq\mathfrak{sp}_{N}\,(\text{resp. }\mathfrak{so}_{N})\,,\eta=-1\,\,(\text{resp. }\eta=1)\,.

We denote by
E_{\alpha,\beta}\in\End V
,
\alpha,\beta\in I
, the elementary matrices:

E_{\alpha,\beta}e_{\gamma}=\delta_{\beta,\gamma}e_{\alpha}
.

Lemma 4.22
.

We have that
(E_{\alpha,\beta})^{\dagger}=\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}E_{\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}
,
\alpha,\beta\in I
.

Proof.

By a straightforward computation we get (
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\eta\in I
)

\displaystyle\langle E_{\alpha,\beta}e_{\gamma}|e_{\eta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta_{\beta,\gamma}\langle e_{\alpha}|e_{\eta}\rangle=\epsilon_{\alpha}\delta_{\beta,\gamma}\delta_{\alpha,\eta^{\prime}}=\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}\epsilon_{\gamma}\delta_{\beta,\gamma}\delta_{\alpha,\eta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}\delta_{\alpha,\eta^{\prime}}\langle e_{\gamma}|e_{\beta^{\prime}}\rangle=\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}\langle e_{\gamma}|E_{\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}e_{\eta}\rangle\,.

The claim follows.
∎

For every
\alpha,\beta\in I
, we let

(4.39)

F_{\alpha,\beta}=E_{\alpha,\beta}-\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}E_{\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}\in\mathfrak{g}\,.

Note also that

(4.40)

F_{\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}=-\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}F_{\alpha,\beta}\,,\qquad\alpha,\beta\in I\,.

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
A=\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in I}a_{\alpha,\beta}E_{\alpha,\beta}\in\End V
.

(a)

A\in\mathfrak{g}
if and only if
a_{\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}=-\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}a_{\alpha,\beta}
, for every

\alpha,\beta\in I
.

(b)

If
A\in\mathfrak{g}
, then

A=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}a_{\alpha,\beta}F_{\alpha,\beta}\,.

Proof.

Straightforward.
∎

The following commutation relations hold (
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\eta\in I
):

(4.41)

[F_{\alpha,\beta},F_{\gamma,\eta}]=\delta_{\gamma,\beta}F_{\alpha,\eta}-\delta_{\eta,\alpha}F_{\beta,\gamma}-\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}\delta_{\alpha^{\prime},\gamma}F_{\beta^{\prime},\eta}+\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}\delta_{\eta,\beta^{\prime}}F_{\gamma,\alpha^{\prime}}\,.

If we depict, as in Section
4.2.1
, the basis elements of
V
as boxes of a symmetric pyramid
associated to the partition
\boldsymbol{p}
(cf. Fig.
1
), let, as usual,

f
be the endomorphism which corresponds to "shifting to the left". Then,
f\in\mathfrak{g}
.
Indeed, using the second property in (
4.36
), we have
f=\sum_{\alpha\leftarrow\beta}E_{\alpha,\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\leftarrow\beta}F_{\alpha,\beta}
.
Note that the "shift to the right" operator
f^{\intercal}
lies in
\mathfrak{g}
as well.

As in Section
4.2.1
, let
h\in\End V
be the diagonal endomorphism of
V

whose eigenvalue on
e_{\alpha}
is the
x
-coordinate of the center of the box labeled by
\alpha
(see Figure
1
) which we denote by
x_{\alpha}
.
We then have the
h
-eigenspace decomposition of
V
(
4.4
) where
D=p_{1}-1
is the maximal eigenvalue of
h
.

Note that
x_{\alpha^{\prime}}=-x_{\alpha}
and we have

h=\sum_{\alpha\in I}x_{\alpha}E_{\alpha,\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in I}x_{\alpha}F_{\alpha,\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}\,.

Clearly, the matrices
F_{\alpha,\beta}
are eigenvectors with respect to the adjoint action of
h
:

(4.42)

(\ad h)F_{\alpha,\beta}=(x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta})F_{\alpha,\beta}\,.

This defines a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of
\mathfrak{g}
,
given by the
\ad h
-eigenspaces as in (
1.1
):

(4.43)

\mathfrak{g}_{k}=\Span_{\mathbb{F}}\{F_{\alpha,\beta}\mid x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}=k\}\,.

Recalling the definition (
4.8
) of the spaces
V_{\pm,i}
,
we have an isomorphism
f^{D_{i}}:V_{-,i}\to V_{+,i}
,

i=1,\dots,s
.
Then the bilinear form (
4.37
) induces non-degenerate bilinear forms

\beta_{i}(\cdot\,,\,\cdot):V_{+,i}\otimes V_{+,i}\to\mathbb{F}
,
i=1,\dots,s
, defined by

(4.44)

\beta_{i}(v,w):=\langle v|f^{D_{i}}w\rangle\,,\quad v,w\in V_{+,i}\,.

Using Lemma
4.21
and the fact that
f^{\dagger}=-f
, we have (
v,w\in V_{+,i}
)

(4.45)

\beta_{i}(v,w)=(-1)^{D_{i}}\eta\beta_{i}(w,v)\,.

4.4.
Integrable triples in
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}_{N}

For
\eta=-1
, the Lie algebra (
4.38
) is
\mathfrak{g}\simeq\mathfrak{sp}_{N}
. In this case the depth of the grading (
4.43
) is
d=2D=2p_{1}-2
. To see this, we describe explicitly the space
\mathfrak{g}_{2D}
(clearly,
\mathfrak{g}_{k}=0
, for
k>2D
).
Let
A\in\Hom(V[-D],V[D])
, and let
\bar{A}

=

Af^{D}

\in

\End(V[D])
.
We have

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(\bar{A}v,w)=\langle Af^{D}v|f^{D}w\rangle=(-1)^{D}\langle v|f^{D}A^{\dagger}f^{D}w\rangle=(-1)^{D}\beta_{1}(v|\overline{A^{\dagger}}w)\,.

Hence, the adjoint of
\bar{A}\in\End V[D]
with respect to
\beta_{1}
is

(4.46)

\bar{A}^{\star}=(-1)^{D}\overline{A^{\dagger}}\,.

As a consequence, we have a bijection

(4.47)

\mathfrak{g}_{2D}\simeq\{B\in\End(V[D])\mid B^{\star}=(-1)^{p_{1}}B\}\,,\quad A\mapsto\bar{A}\,.

Using equations (
4.45
) and (
4.47
) we have that, if
p_{1}
is even, then
\mathfrak{g}_{d}
is identified with the space of selfadjont (with respect to the symmetric bilinear form
\beta_{1}
in (
4.44
)) endomorphsims of
V[D]
, while if
p_{1}
is odd then
\mathfrak{g}_{d}\simeq\mathfrak{sp}(V[D],\beta_{1})
.

4.4.1.
The centralizer
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})

From Lemma
4.23
we have that
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{k}
can be decomposed as

(4.48)

E=\sum_{x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}=k}c_{\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\beta}\,,\quad c_{\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}=-\epsilon_{\alpha}\epsilon_{\beta}c_{\alpha,\beta}\,.

Proposition 4.24
.

The centralizer of
\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2}
in
\mathfrak{g}
is
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})=W\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d}
, where

W=\Span_{\mathbb{F}}\{F_{\alpha\beta}\mid x_{\alpha}=D-1,x_{\beta}=-D+1\}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{d-2}\,.

Proof.

As in Lemma
4.6
we have

\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 0}
.
Let
\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}\in I
be such that
x_{\tilde{\alpha}}=D
and

x_{\tilde{\beta}}\leqslant D-2
(the box
\tilde{\beta}
is completely at the left of the box
\tilde{\alpha}
).
Then
F_{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2}
. Hence, letting
E
as in (
4.48
),
and using the commutation relations (
4.41
) and equation (
4.40
) we get

(4.49)

0=[F_{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}},E]=\sum_{x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}=k}c_{\alpha\beta}[F_{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}},F_{\alpha\beta}]=\sum_{x_{\tilde{\beta}}-x_{\beta}=k}2c_{\tilde{\beta}\beta}F_{\tilde{\alpha}\beta}-\sum_{x_{\alpha}-D=k}2c_{\alpha\tilde{\alpha}}F_{\alpha\tilde{\beta}}\,.

If
k\geqslant 1
, then the condition
x_{\alpha}-D=k\geqslant 1
is empty.
Hence,

\sum_{x_{\tilde{\beta}}-x_{\beta}=k}c_{\tilde{\beta}\beta}F_{\tilde{\alpha}\beta}=0\,,

which implies that that
c_{\alpha\beta}=0
if
x_{\alpha}\leqslant D-2
.
Using the second equation in (
4.48
), we have also that
c_{\alpha\beta}=0
if
x_{\beta}\geqslant-D+2
.
If
k=0
, a similar argument to the one used in the proof of Lemma
4.6
shows that equation (
4.49
) implies

E=0
. As a consequence,
E\in W\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d}
. On the other hand, by Proposition

4.7
, we have

W\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d}=\mathfrak{z}((\mathfrak{gl}_{N})_{\geqslant 2})\cap\mathfrak{g}\subset\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})\,.

∎

Recalling the definition of integrable triples given in the Introduction, by Proposition
4.24
,
if
E
is an integrable element for
f
, then

E\in\left(W\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d}\right)\cap\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 1}
(note that
\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 1}
for
p_{1}\geqslant 2
, and
W\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 1}
for
p_{1}\geqslant 3
).

4.4.2.
The coisotropy condition

An element
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
can be uniquely written as
E=A-A^{\dagger}
,
where
A\in\Hom(V[-D+1],V[D])
.
Let

(4.50)

a=Af^{D-1}A^{\dagger}f^{D}\in\End(V[D]).

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
. The subspace
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is
coisotropic with respect to the bilinear form (
1.2
) if and only if
a=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.3
with
B=-A^{\dagger}
,
we have that

\ad E\circ(\ad f)^{-1}\circ\ad E|_{\mathfrak{g}_{-d}}=0
if and only if

[a,(f^{\intercal})^{D}U]=0\,,\quad\text{for every }U\in\mathfrak{g}_{-d}\,.

Note that
\mathfrak{g}_{-d}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{d}
and that
(f^{\intercal})^{D}\mathfrak{g}_{-d}\subset\End(V[D])
.
By the description of
\mathfrak{g}_{d}
given in (
4.47
) we have that
(f^{\intercal})^{D}\mathfrak{g}_{-d}=\{B\in\End(V[D])\mid B^{\star}=(-1)^{p_{1}}B\}
,
where the adjoint is with respect to the bilinear form
\beta_{1}
defined in (
4.44
).
Recall that
a\in\End(V[D])
.
Using equation (
4.46
), its adjoint with respect to the bilinear form
\beta_{1}

is

a^{\star}=(-1^{D})(Af^{D-1}A^{\dagger})^{\dagger}f^{D}=-a\,.

Hence, by (
4.47
), if
p_{1}
is odd, then
a=0
since it lies in the center of
\mathfrak{sp}(V[D],\beta_{1})
. On the other hand,
if
p_{1}
is even,
a=0
since it is a skewadjoint operator commuting with all selfadjoint operators.
∎

Now, let
E\in W=\Span_{\mathbb{F}}\{F_{\alpha\beta}\mid x_{\alpha}=D-1,x_{\beta}=-D+1\}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{d-2}
.
As in Section
4.2.3
we write
E=c(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}
, where
c\in\End(V[D-1])
.

Proposition 4.26
.

Let
E=c(f^{\intercal})^{D-1}\in W
. The subspace
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is
coisotropic with respect to the bilinear form (
1.2
) if and only if

c^{2}=0
.

Proof.

Same as the proof of Proposition
4.9
.
∎

4.4.3.
Integrable
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}

Recall from Section
4.2.4
that
E=u(f^{\intercal})^{D}
, where
u\in\End(V[D])
.
The following result follows from Lemmas
4.10
and
4.11
.

Proposition 4.27
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}_{N}
and
f
its nonzero nilpotent element of depth
d
. Then
E=u(f^{\intercal})^{D}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}
is integrable for
f
if and only if
u
is semisimple.

4.4.4.
No integrable elements for
f
in
\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus W

Recall the element
a
, defined by (
4.50
) for
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
, and the element
c
, defined by (
4.22
) for
E\in W
.

Proposition 4.28
.

There are no integrable elements for
f
in
\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus W
.

Proof.

By contradiction, let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
be an integrable element for
f
.
Since
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is coisotropic, by Proposition
4.25
, we have
a=0
.
Clearly,
f+E=(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\leqslant 2}}+(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 3}}
, and
f+E
preserves the direct sum decomposition

V=V_{\leqslant 2}\oplus V_{\geqslant 3}
. Note that
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 3}}=f\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\geqslant 3}}
is nilpotent.
On the other hand it is not difficult to check that
(f+E)^{2D-1}v=av=0
for every
v\in V_{\leqslant 2}
.
Hence,

(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{\leqslant 2}}
is nilpotent as well. This proves that
f+E
is nilpotent, contradicting the fact that it is integrable.
The proof of the claim for
E\in W
is the same as the proof of Proposition
4.16
.
∎

As a consequence of Propositions
4.27
and
4.28
we get the following.

Corollary 4.29
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}_{N}
and
f
its non-zero nilpotent element. The element
f+E
, where
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{k}
,
k\geqslant 1
, is integrable if and only if
f+E
is an integrable cyclic element.

4.5.
Integrable triples in
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}_{N}
for nilpotent elements of even depth

Recall that for
\eta=1
in (
4.36
), the algebra (
4.38
) is
\mathfrak{g}\simeq\mathfrak{so}_{N}
.

Lemma 4.30

(cf.
[
EKV13
]
)
.

The depth
d
of the grading (
1.1
) for
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}_{N}
is

(i)

d=2D
for
r_{1}\geqslant 2
;

(ii)

d=2D-1
for
p_{1}
odd,
r_{1}=1
,
p_{2}=p_{1}-1
;

(iii)

d=2D-2
for
p_{1}
odd,
r_{1}=1
,
p_{2}\leqslant p_{1}-2
.

Proof.

As in Section
4.4
, let
A\in\Hom(V[-D],V[D])
,
D=p_{1}-1
, and consider
\bar{A}=Af^{D}\in\End(V[D])
.
Then, the adjoint of
\bar{A}
with respect to the bilinear form
\beta_{1}
defined in (
4.44
) is given by equation (
4.46
) and the space
\mathfrak{g}_{2D}
is described in
(
4.47
).
Using equation (
4.45
) we have that, if
p_{1}
is even (hence
r_{1}
is even), then
\mathfrak{g}_{2D}
is identified with the space of selfadjont (with respect to the skewsymmetric bilinear form
\beta_{1}
) endomorphisms of
V[D]
, while if
p_{1}
is odd then
\mathfrak{g}_{2D}\simeq\mathfrak{so}(V[D],\beta_{1})
.
It follows that the depth of the grading (
4.43
) is
d=2D
when

r_{1}=\dim V[D]\geqslant 2
, proving case (i).

If
r_{1}=1
(hence
p_{1}
is odd), then
\mathfrak{g}_{2D}=\{0\}
and
d<2D
. Consider first the case when

p_{2}=p_{1}-1
. In this case,
V[D]
is one dimensional and
V[-D+1]
is an
r_{2}
-dimensional vector space.
Furthermore,
\mathfrak{g}_{2D-1}=\{A-A^{\dagger}\mid A\in\Hom(V[-D+1],V[D])\}\simeq V[-D+1]^{*}
. Hence,
in this case the depth of the grading (
4.43
) is
d=2D-1
, proving case (ii).

We are left to consider the case
r_{1}=1
and
p_{2}\leqslant p_{1}-2
. In this case
d=2D-2
.
Indeed,
F_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{p_{1}-1}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{2D-2}
, where
\alpha_{1}
is the rightmost box of the pyramid (which is in the bottom row) and
\alpha_{p_{1}-1}
is the second leftmost box of the bottom row, is a non-zero element.
∎

4.5.1.
Even depth
d=2D

Proposition 4.31
.

We have that
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})=W\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d}
,
where

W=\Span_{\mathbb{F}}\{F_{\alpha\beta}\mid x_{\alpha}=D-1,x_{\beta}=-D+1\}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{d-2}\,.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of Proposition
4.24
.
∎

Proposition 4.32
.

(i)

Let, as in Section
4.4.2
,
E=A-A^{\dagger}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
, with

A\in\Hom(V[-D],V[D-1])
, and let
a=A^{\dagger}f^{D-1}Af^{D}\in\End(V[D])
.
If
p_{1}
is even and
r_{1}=2
, then the subspace
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is
coisotropic with respect to the bilinear form (
1.2
). In the other cases,

\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is
coisotropic if and only if
a=0
.

(ii)

Let
E\in W
and let, as in Section
4.4.2
,
c=Ef^{D-1}\in\End(V[D-1])
. The subspace
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is
coisotropic with respect to the bilinear form (
1.2
) if and only if
c^{2}=0
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
4.25
we have that

\ad E\circ(\ad f)^{-1}\circ\ad E|_{\mathfrak{g}_{-d}}=0
if and only if
[a,(f^{\intercal})^{D}U]=0
,
for every
U\in\mathfrak{g}_{-d}
and that
a\in\End(V[D])
is skewadjoint with respect to the bilinear form
(
4.44
). Note that
\mathfrak{g}_{-d}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{d}
and that, by equation (
4.47
),
we have
(f^{\intercal})^{D}\mathfrak{g}_{-d}=\{B\in\End(V[D])\mid B^{\star}=(-1)^{p_{1}}B\}\End(V[D])\subset\End(V[D])
.
If
p_{1}
is odd, then
a=0
since it lies in the center of
\mathfrak{so}(V[D],\beta_{1})
.
On the other hand, if
p_{1}
is even (this implies
r_{1}
even), then

a
is a skewadjoint operator (with respect to a skewsymmetric bilinear form) commuting with all selfadjoint operators with respect to the same bilinear form. This gives no condition on
a
when
r_{1}=2
, but implies that
a=0
for
r_{1}>2
. This proves part i). The proof of part ii) is similar to the proof of
Proposition
4.9
.
∎

Let
p_{1}
be even and
r_{1}=2=\dim(V[D])
.
Let us choose a basis
\{u,v\}
of
V[D]
such that

\beta_{1}(u,v)=\langle u|f^{D}v\rangle=1\,,

where the bilinear form
\beta_{1}
on
V[D]
defined in (
4.44
) is skew-symmetric by (
4.45
).
Then, clearly
\{f^{k}u,f^{h}v\mid 0\leqslant h,k\leqslant D\}
is a basis of
V_{1}
. In particular
V[-D]=\mathbb{F}f^{D}u\oplus\mathbb{F}f^{D}v
.

For every
w\in V
, let us denote by
\phi(w):V\to\mathbb{F}
the linear functional
\phi(w)(w_{1})=\langle w|w_{1}\rangle
,
w_{1}\in V
. Hence, we can write

(4.51)

A=x\phi(v)+y\phi(u)\,,

for some
x,y\in V[D-1]
. Then

(4.52)

A^{\dagger}=u\phi(y)+v\phi(x)\,.

Indeed, using the fact that
\beta_{1}(w_{1},w_{1})=0
, for every
w_{1}\in V[D]
we have

\langle Af^{D}u|w\rangle=-\langle x|w\rangle=\langle f^{D}u|A^{\dagger}w\rangle\,,\quad\langle Af^{D}v|w\rangle=\langle y|w\rangle=\langle f^{D}v|A^{\dagger}w\rangle\,.

Hence,
in this case,
E
can be uniquely written as

(4.53)

E=x\phi(v)+y\phi(u)-u\phi(y)-v\phi(x)\,,\quad x,y\in V[D-1]\,.

Lemma 4.33
.

With respect to the basis
\{u,v\}
of
V[D]
we have that

(4.54)

a=A^{\dagger}f^{D-1}Af^{D}=\begin{pmatrix}-\beta_{2}(x,y)&\beta_{2}(y,y)\\
-\beta_{2}(x,x)&\beta_{2}(x,y)\end{pmatrix}\,,

where
\beta_{2}
is defined by (
4.44
).

Proof.

We have, by (
4.51
) and (
4.52
),

\displaystyle a(u)

\displaystyle=A^{\dagger}f^{D-1}Af^{D}u=A^{\dagger}f^{D-1}\left(\langle v|f^{D}u\rangle x+\langle u|f^{D}u\rangle y\right)

\displaystyle=-A^{\dagger}f^{D-1}x=-\left(\langle y|f^{D-1}x\rangle u+\langle xf^{D-1}x\rangle v\right)

\displaystyle=-\beta_{2}(y,x)u-\beta_{2}(x,x)v\,,

which gives the first coloumn in the matrix (
4.54
). Similarly for the second column.
∎

Proposition 4.34
.

Let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
be as in (
4.53
) and let
a\in\End(V[D])
be as in (
4.54
). If
a
is a non-zero semisimple element, then
(f+E)_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-2}
.

Proof.

By assumption,
a
is a non-zero semisimple element.
In (
4.54
)
a
is represented by a
2\times 2
traceless matrix. Hence, it is non-zero semisimple if and only if
\det(a)\neq 0
. This implies that
x
and
y
are linearly independent.
Let

\tilde{U}=\{w\in V[D-1]\mid\beta_{2}(x,w)=\beta_{2}(y,w)=0\}\subset V[D-1]\,.

Since
\beta_{2}
is non-degenerate and
\det(a)\neq 0
it easily follows that

V[D-1]=\mathbb{F}x\oplus\mathbb{F}y\oplus\tilde{U}\,.

As a consequence, applying
f
repeatedly, we get

V_{2}=V_{x}\oplus V_{y}\oplus U\,,

where

V_{x}=\oplus_{k=0}^{D-1}\mathbb{F}f^{k}x\,,\quad V_{y}=\oplus_{k=0}^{D-1}\mathbb{F}f^{k}y\,,\quad U=\oplus_{k=0}^{D-1}f^{k}\tilde{U}\,.

Note that
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{U\oplus V_{\geqslant 3}}\!\!=\!\!f\mathbbm{1}_{U\oplus V_{\geqslant 3}}
is nilpotent
and commutes with
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}\oplus V_{x}\oplus V_{y}}
.
Moreover, it is not difficult to check that

(4.55)

(f+E)^{2D+1}w=aw\,,\qquad w\in V_{1}\oplus V_{x}\oplus V_{y}\,.

Let
q(t)
denote the minimal polynomial of
a
, which has non-zero distinct roots since
a
is non-zero semisimple.
Equation (
4.55
) implies that
the minimal polynomial of
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}\oplus V_{x}\oplus V_{y}}
divides
q(t^{2D+1})
which obviously has also
distinct roots. Then
(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}\oplus V_{x}\oplus V_{y}}
is semisimple.
In conclusion, the Jordan decomposition of
f+E
has
(f+E)_{s}=(f+E)\mathbbm{1}_{V_{1}\oplus V_{x}\oplus V_{y}}

and
(f+E)_{n}=f\mathbbm{1}_{U\oplus V_{\geqslant 3}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{-2}
.
∎

The next result characterizes integrable elements for
\mathfrak{so}_{N}
when the depth of the grading is

d=2D
.

Theorem 4.35
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}_{N}
and let
f\in\mathfrak{g}
be a non-zero nilpotent element of depth
d=2D
,
D=p_{1}-1
.

(i)

Let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}
and let, as in Section
4.2.4
,
u=Ef^{D}\in\End(V[D])
. Then
E
is integrable for
f
if and only if
u
is semisimple.

(ii)

Let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
and let, as above
E=A-A^{\dagger}
, where
A\in\Hom(V[-D],V[D-1])
,
and
a=A^{\dagger}f^{D-1}Af^{D}\in\End(V[D])
. Then
E
is integrable for
f
if and only if
p_{1}
is even,
r_{1}=2
and
\det a\neq 0
.

(iii)

If
E\in W
, then
E
cannot be integrable for
f
.

Proof.

Part i) follows by Proposition
4.12
. If
p_{1}
is even and
r_{1}=2
part ii) follows from
Proposition
4.32
i), Lemma
4.15
and Proposition
4.34
.
The remaining claim in part ii) and iii) can be proved in the same way as for the proof of Proposition
4.28
using Proposition
4.32
.
∎

Remark 4.36
.

We have a non-zero
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
if and only if
p_{2}=p_{1}-1
(otherwise
\dim\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}=0
).
In this case, if
r_{2}=1
, there are no elements
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
satisfying the assumption of Theorem

4.35
ii). Indeed, since
\dim V[D-1]=r_{2}=1
,
x
and
y
are linearly dependent thus

a
defined in (
4.54
) is nilpotent.

4.5.2.
Even depth
d=2D-2

Proposition 4.37
.

The centralizer of
\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2}
in
\mathfrak{g}
is
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})=\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d}
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 0}
. By degree considerations,
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2})\supset\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d}
.
On the other hand, let
1\in I
be the label of the rightmost box of the pyramid associated to
\boldsymbol{p}
(note that
x_{1}=D
) and
p_{1}\in I
be the label of the leftmost box (note that
x_{p_{1}}=-D
).
Let also
\tilde{\beta}\in I
be such that
x_{\tilde{\beta}}\leqslant D-2
(the box
\tilde{\beta}
is completely at the left of the box
1
).
Then
F_{1\tilde{\beta}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 2}
. Hence, letting
E
as in equation (
4.48
),
using the commutation relations (
4.41
), the second equation in (
4.48
) and (
4.40
), we have that

(4.56)

0=[F_{1\tilde{\beta}},E]=\sum_{x_{\alpha}-x_{\beta}=k}c_{\alpha\beta}[F_{1\tilde{\beta}},F_{\alpha\beta}]=\sum_{x_{\tilde{\beta}}-x_{\beta}=k}2c_{\tilde{\beta}\beta}F_{1\beta}-\sum_{x_{\alpha}-D=k}2c_{\alpha 1}F_{\alpha\tilde{\beta}}\,.

If
k\geqslant 1
, then the condition
x_{\alpha}-D=k\geqslant 1
implies that
x_{\alpha}\geqslant 1+D
, which is empty.
Hence,
c_{\alpha\beta}=0
if
x_{\alpha}\leqslant D-2
and
\beta\neq p_{1}
(since

F_{1,p_{1}}=0
by (
4.40
) and (
4.36
)).
Using the second equation in (
4.48
), we have also that
c_{\alpha\beta}=0
if
\alpha\neq 1
and
x_{\beta}\geqslant-D+2
.
Since
V[D-1]=V[-D+1]=0
, we have

E=\sum_{x_{\beta}=D-k}c_{1\beta}F_{1\beta}\,.

Let then assume that

\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}\in I
are such that
x_{\tilde{\alpha}}-x_{\tilde{\beta}}\geqslant 2
. Then

(4.57)

0=[F_{\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}},E]=2\delta_{x_{\tilde{\alpha}},D-k}c_{1,\tilde{\alpha}}F_{1,\tilde{\beta}}\,.

If
x_{\tilde{\alpha}}=D-k
, then
-D\leqslant x_{\tilde{\beta}}\leqslant D-2-k
. Hence, equation (
4.57
)
implies that
c_{1\beta}=0
for
x_{\beta}>-D+3
, thus showing that
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
.

If
k=0
, a similar argument to the one used in the proof of Lemma
4.6
shows that
E=0
.
∎

In the sequel, we are going to use the following basis of
V_{1}
. Let
v_{+}\in V_{+,1}
be such that

\beta_{1}(v_{+},v_{+})=1
. Then we consider the basis
\{f^{k}v_{+}\}_{k=0}^{D}
of

V_{1}
. We also denote
v_{-}=f^{D}v_{+}
.

An element
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}
can be uniquely written as
E=A-A^{\dagger}
, for some

A

\in

\Hom(V[-D],V[D-2])
.
Note that
V[D]=\mathbb{F}v_{+}
and
V[D-2]=\mathbb{F}fv_{+}\oplus V_{+,2}
. Hence,

(4.58)

E=(a+\lambda fv_{+})\phi(v_{+})-v_{+}(\phi(a)+\lambda\phi(fv_{+}))\,,

for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
and
a\in V_{+,2}
.

Let now
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
instead. It can be uniquely written as
E=B-B^{\dagger}
, where
B\in\Hom(V[-D],V_{+,3})
. Hence,
B=b\phi(v_{+})
,
for some
b\in V_{+,3}
, and we can write

(4.59)

E=b\phi(v_{+})-v_{+}\phi(b)\,.

We also set

(4.60)

\beta=\langle b|f^{D-3}b\rangle\,.

Proposition 4.38
.

Let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
be as in (
4.59
) and let
\beta
be as in (
4.60
). The subspace
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is
coisotropic with respect to the bilinear form (
1.2
) if and only if
\beta=0
.

Proof.

Let
E=B-B^{\dagger}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
and let
U=X-X^{\dagger}\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}
, for some
X

\in

\Hom(V[D],V[-D+2])
.
Recall that
B=b\phi(v_{+})
. Hence,
\mathbbm{1}_{fV}B=0
. Moreover,
B^{\dagger}=v_{+}\phi(b)
, Hence,
B^{\dagger}\mathbbm{1}_{f^{\intercal}V}=0
.
By Proposition
3.13
and Lemma
4.5
we have that
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is coisotropic if and only if

(4.61)

(f^{\intercal})^{D-2}XB^{\dagger}f^{D-3}B=B^{\dagger}f^{D-3}AX^{\dagger}(f^{\intercal})^{D-2}=0\,,

for every
X\in\Hom(V[D],V[-D+2])
. Note that the middle term in equation in (
4.61
) is the adjoint of the first term.
Hence,
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
is coisotropic if and only if
(f^{\intercal})^{D-2}XB^{\dagger}f^{D-3}B=0
for every
X\in\Hom(V[D],V[-D+2])
.
Then, we have

0=\left((f^{\intercal})^{D-2}XB^{\dagger}f^{D-3}B\right)(v_{-})=\beta(f^{\intercal})^{D-2}X(v_{+})\,.

Since
X
is arbitrary we get
\beta=0
.
∎

The next result characterizes integrable elements for
\mathfrak{so}_{N}
when the depth of the grading is

d=2D-2
.

Theorem 4.39
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}_{N}
and let
f\in\mathfrak{g}
be a nilpotent element of even depth
d=2D-2
,
D=p_{1}-1
.

(i)

Let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d}
be as in (
4.58
), where
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
and
a\in V_{+,2}
, and let also

\alpha=\langle a|f^{D-2}a\rangle
.
E
is integrable for
f
if and only if
either
\alpha\neq 0
, or
\alpha=a=0
and
\lambda\neq 0
.

(ii)

There are no integrable elements for
f
in
\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
.

Proof.

In order to prove part (i) one can use the same arguments that will be used for the proof Theorem
5.6
(
ii
) (in fact, part (i) of the present Theorem corresponds to the special case
b=0
of Theorem
5.6
(
ii
)).
Let us then prove part (ii).
Let
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
be as in (
4.59
) and let
\beta
be as in (
4.60
). If
E
is integrable for
f
, then
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{E}
must be coisotropic. By Proposition
4.38
, then
\beta=0
, and, as one can easily check,
f+E
is nilpotent. This contradicts the fact that
E
is integrable and proves part (ii).
∎

Remark 4.40
.

Theorem
4.39
(
i
) follows from Theorem
3.15
since in this case
Z(\mathfrak{s})|\mathfrak{g}_{d}=\st(SO_{r_{2}})\oplus\mathbbm{1}
(see Table
1
).

,)

and

is a Cartan subspace.

For

•

For

).

Theorem 4.41
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}_{N}
, let
f\in\mathfrak{g}
be a non-zero nilpotent element of even depth
d
and let
\boldsymbol{p}=(p_{1}^{(r_{1})},p_{2}^{(r_{2})},...)
be the corresponding partition. Then

(a)

All the linear groups
Z(\mathfrak{s})|\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
are polar, and described in Table
2
.

(b)

There exists an integrable quasi-cyclic element for
f
if and only if
p_{1}
is even,
r_{1}=2
, and
p_{2}=p_{1}-1
,
r_{2}\geqslant 2
. Such an element is the unique, up to equivalence, element
f+E
, where
E\in\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
is semisimple with respect to
Z(\mathfrak{s})
.

Proof.

(
a
) follows from Table
2
, since all these linear groups are theta groups.

In order to prove (
b
), note that in the case in question,
Z(\mathfrak{s})|\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}=\st(Sp_{2})\otimes\st(SO_{r_{2}})
, for which the rank equals
1
. This shows that, up to equivalence, there is at most one integrable quasi-cyclic element. Its existence follows from Theorem
4.35
(
ii
).
∎

Table 4.
Odd nilpotent elements
f
of even depth in exceptional
\mathfrak{g}
, for which
Z(\mathfrak{s})|\mathfrak{g}_{d-1}
has only trivial invariants

4.6.1.
Nilpotent elements with label
{\mathrm{A}}_{1}

A representative for the orbit with label
{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
is given by a negative root vector
e_{-\alpha}
— arbitrary for type
{\mathrm{E}}
and a long one for
{\mathrm{F}}_{4}
and
{\mathrm{G}}_{2}
.
Depth is
2
, and a
1
-dimensional Cartan subspace for
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{s})|\mathfrak{g}_{1}
is spanned by the vector
E=v^{*}+v_{*}
,
where
v^{*}
, resp.
v_{*}
is the highest, resp. lowest weight vector.
Moreover
E
satisfies the coisotropy condition, and the quasi-cyclic element
f+E
is semisimple.

4.6.2.
Nilpotent element with label
\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
in
{\mathrm{F}}_{4}

A representative
f
for
\widetilde{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
is given by a short root vector.
Depth is
2
, with
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
of dimension
8
. The representation
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{s})|\mathfrak{g}_{1}
is the direct sum
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
of two standard representations of
\mathfrak{sl}_{4}
,
and a Cartan subspace is spanned by
E=v^{1}+v_{2}
, where
v^{1}
is a highest weight vector for
V_{1}
and
v_{2}
a lowest weight vector for
V_{2}
.
This
E
does not satisfy the coisotropy condition, which implies that all quasi-cyclic elements are nilpotent.

4.6.3.
Nilpotent element with label
2{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
in
{\mathrm{E}}_{6}

A representative
f
for
2{\mathrm{A}}_{1}
is given by the sum of any two commuting root vectors.
Depth is
2
, and
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{s})|\mathfrak{g}_{1}
is as in Example
2.10
,
so we can choose a basis
\{E_{1},E_{2}\}
of a Cartan subspace as there, with
E_{1}=v^{+}+v_{-}
and
E_{2}=v^{-}+v_{+}
.
Coisotropy condition for
E=x_{1}E_{1}+x_{2}E_{2}
turns out to be
x_{1}^{2}=x_{2}^{2}
.
We then check that both
f+x(E_{1}+E_{2})
and
f+x(E_{1}-E_{2})
are semisimple for
x\neq 0
.
These are all integrable quasi-cyclic elements
f+E
for
E
from the Cartan subspace.

In fact these two solutions
E_{1}+E_{2}
and
E_{1}-E_{2}
are equivalent under the action of
Z(\mathfrak{s})
. This can be seen as follows. Take

Consider the element
H
of the Cartan subalgebra determined by the following values of simple roots on it:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&2&0&\!\!\!-2&0\\
&&0\end{array}

Its eigenvalues on
\mathfrak{g}
are
-2,0,2
, so it defines a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{-2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}
of
\mathfrak{g}
. Moreover
f,E_{1}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
,
